### Safer Skin Lesion Classification with Global Class Activation Probability Map Evaluation and SafeML
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20776v1)
- **Authors**: Kuniko Paxton, Koorosh Aslansefat, Amila Akagić, Dhavalkumar Thakker, Yiannis Papadopoulos
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in skin lesion classification models have significantly improved accuracy, with some models even surpassing dermatologists' diagnostic performance. However, in medical practice, distrust in AI models remains a challenge. Beyond high accuracy, trustworthy, explainable diagnoses are essential. Existing explainability methods have reliability issues, with LIME-based methods suffering from inconsistency, while CAM-based methods failing to consider all classes. To address these limitations, we propose Global Class Activation Probabilistic Map Evaluation, a method that analyses all classes' activation probability maps probabilistically and at a pixel level. By visualizing the diagnostic process in a unified manner, it helps reduce the risk of misdiagnosis. Furthermore, the application of SafeML enhances the detection of false diagnoses and issues warnings to doctors and patients as needed, improving diagnostic reliability and ultimately patient safety. We evaluated our method using the ISIC datasets with MobileNetV2 and Vision Transformers.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Safer Skin Lesion Classification with Global Class Activation Probability Map Evaluation and SafeML":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new method, Global Class Activation Probability Map Evaluation (GCAPM), to improve the reliability and explainability of skin lesion classification models. It addresses limitations in existing explainability techniques like LIME and CAM-based methods, arguing that they don't adequately consider all classes and can lead to misleading visualizations.  GCAPM analyzes activation regions across all classes probabilistically at a pixel level, normalizing and aggregating class activation maps.  The authors integrate GCAPM with SafeML, a framework for runtime model monitoring, to detect abnormal predictions and prompt human intervention when necessary. They evaluate their method using ISIC datasets with MobileNetV2 and Vision Transformers, showing improved explainability, better risk assessment, and enhanced detection of abnormal diagnoses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a reasonable degree of novelty, although not revolutionary.

*   **GCAPM:** While CAM-based methods are well-established, the probabilistic, all-class focused aggregation and visualization provided by GCAPM is a worthwhile contribution. It helps address some existing limitations regarding reliability and the potentially misleading nature of explanations focused solely on the predicted class.
*   **Integration with SafeML:**  The combination of GCAPM with SafeML for active monitoring is a useful extension of existing SafeML principles into the image classification domain. The integration with the concept of distributional drift as described in the paper does adds another layer of safety.
*   **Selective Prediction:** The use of attribute metrics from the explanation to improve detection of unreliable predictions is also novel.

However, the core ideas borrow heavily from existing CAM and SafeML concepts, rather than introducing fundamentally new paradigms.

**Significance:**

The potential significance of the work lies in its focus on building trustworthy AI systems for medical diagnosis.
*   **Improved Trust:** By addressing limitations in existing explainability methods, the paper aims to increase clinicians' trust in AI models, which is crucial for wider adoption.
*   **Enhanced Safety:** The integration of SafeML principles helps mitigate risks associated with misdiagnosis and improves patient safety, especially in self-diagnosis tools.
*   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The paper uses quantitative metrics (Attribute Sensitivity, Attribute False Positive Rate) to assess the quality of explanations and correlate them with model performance, which is a step forward in making explainability more rigorous.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing explainability methods in the context of medical diagnosis.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** GCAPM addresses several issues – it is not only an explanation, it combines SafeML principles for robust validation.
*   **Experimental Validation:**  The authors provide experimental results using benchmark datasets and common architectures, demonstrating the effectiveness of their approach.
*   **Focus on Real-World Applications:** The integration with mobile/web self-diagnosis tools increases the real-world applicability and impact.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The GCAPM method, while a worthwhile improvement, is incremental rather than transformative. It builds upon existing CAM techniques and addresses specific limitations.
*   **Reliance on Segmentation:**  The evaluation relies on pre-existing or generated segmentation masks. The accuracy of these masks will directly impact the evaluation metrics, introducing a potential source of error. While using Deeplabv3 ensures reasonable accuracy on ISIC datasets, a discussion on uncertainty in ground truth would improve the paper.
*   **Complexity:** The approach adds complexity to the model evaluation pipeline.
*   **Limited Generalization Discussion:**  There's limited discussion on how GCAPM and SafeML would generalize to different types of medical images or other diagnostic tasks. There is also minimal discussion on real world deployment, such as computational cost or limitations of the work.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of explainable AI in medical imaging, particularly in the context of skin lesion classification. The GCAPM method addresses some key limitations of existing techniques and is integrated with SafeML to enhance model reliability and safety. The experimental results support the effectiveness of the approach. However, the novelty is incremental, and there are some limitations in the evaluation and generalization discussion.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

*   A score of 7 reflects the combination of worthwhile novelty, strong experimental validation, and potential for impact in the medical field.
*   It acknowledges that the core ideas (CAM, SafeML) are not entirely new, but the specific combination and refinements are valuable.
*   The score reflects the need for further research to address the limitations mentioned above, such as the dependence on segmentation masks and generalization to other domains. A higher score would require more breakthrough, novel concepts.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Compositional Generalisation in VLMs and Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20783v1)
- **Authors**: Beth Pearson, Bilal Boulbarss, Michael Wray, Martha Lewis
- **Abstract**: A fundamental aspect of the semantics of natural language is that novel meanings can be formed from the composition of previously known parts. Vision-language models (VLMs) have made significant progress in recent years, however, there is evidence that they are unable to perform this kind of composition. For example, given an image of a red cube and a blue cylinder, a VLM such as CLIP is likely to incorrectly label the image as a red cylinder or a blue cube, indicating it represents the image as a `bag-of-words' and fails to capture compositional semantics. Diffusion models have recently gained significant attention for their impressive generative abilities, and zero-shot classifiers based on diffusion models have been shown to perform competitively with CLIP in certain compositional tasks. In this work we explore whether the generative Diffusion Classifier has improved compositional generalisation abilities compared to discriminative models. We assess three models -- Diffusion Classifier, CLIP, and ViLT -- on their ability to bind objects with attributes and relations in both zero-shot learning (ZSL) and generalised zero-shot learning (GZSL) settings. Our results show that the Diffusion Classifier and ViLT perform well at concept binding tasks, but that all models struggle significantly with the relational GZSL task, underscoring the broader challenges VLMs face with relational reasoning. Analysis of CLIP embeddings suggests that the difficulty may stem from overly similar representations of relational concepts such as left and right. Code and dataset are available at: https://github.com/otmive/diffusion_classifier_clip
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the compositional generalization abilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Diffusion Models, specifically examining their capacity to bind objects with attributes and relationships.  The authors evaluate Diffusion Classifier, CLIP, and ViLT on a modified version of the Concept Binding Benchmark in both zero-shot learning (ZSL) and generalized zero-shot learning (GZSL) settings. Their results indicate that while Diffusion Classifier and ViLT perform well at binding objects and attributes, all models struggle with the relational GZSL task, suggesting a fundamental challenge in relational reasoning for VLMs. The authors further analyze CLIP embeddings, pointing to overly similar representations of relational concepts (like "left" and "right") as a potential reason for the difficulty.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a useful comparative analysis of different models on a compositional task. While the concept of evaluating compositional generalization isn't entirely novel (many papers have tackled this), the specific comparison between Diffusion Classifiers and more established VLMs like CLIP and ViLT adds value. The investigation into *why* models fail, specifically the CLIP embedding analysis relating to relational concepts, is a worthwhile contribution. However, the benchmark's reliance on synthetic images, while controlled, reduces the real-world impact of the analysis. It’s an incremental advance, building upon existing work.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights persistent limitations of VLMs in relational reasoning, even with recent advancements like Diffusion Classifiers. This underscores the need for better architectural designs and training strategies that explicitly address compositional understanding, especially in relational contexts. The identification of issues related to the representation of spatial relationships can inform future research directions. It contributes to understanding the capabilities and limitations of these models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined experimental setup with clear evaluation metrics.
    *   Comparative study of multiple models (CLIP, ViLT, Diffusion Classifier).
    *   Analysis of model performance in both ZSL and GZSL settings.
    *   Insightful analysis of CLIP embeddings to explain relational reasoning failures.
    *   The creation and extension of the Concept Binding Benchmark.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The benchmark relies solely on synthetic data which may not generalize to real-world scenarios.
    *   The analysis section could be expanded to explore the reasons for the poor performance of CLIP with regards to GZSL.
    *   The contribution is somewhat incremental, building on existing literature.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is unlikely to revolutionize the field but has potential to influence researchers working on compositional generalization in VLMs by demonstrating and quantifying the persistent challenge of relational reasoning. The CLIP embedding analysis provides a concrete direction for improving models.

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid experimental analysis of compositional generalization in VLMs. It uses a carefully controlled synthetic dataset, which allows for precise measurement of specific capabilities. However, the synthetic nature of the data does limit the real-world relevance of the findings. The comparative analysis of CLIP, ViLT, and Diffusion Classifiers is valuable, but not groundbreaking. The investigation into the CLIP embedding space is a notable contribution, shedding light on the model's difficulties in representing relational concepts. Overall, while the paper lacks transformative novelty, it makes a worthwhile contribution to our understanding of compositional limitations in VLMs.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Exploring Machine Learning and Language Models for Multimodal Depression Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20805v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Si Zhao Hong, Timothy Zoe Delaya, Sherwyn Chan Yin Kit, Pai Chet Ng, Xiaoxiao Miao
- **Abstract**: This paper presents our approach to the first Multimodal Personality-Aware Depression Detection Challenge, focusing on multimodal depression detection using machine learning and deep learning models. We explore and compare the performance of XGBoost, transformer-based architectures, and large language models (LLMs) on audio, video, and text features. Our results highlight the strengths and limitations of each type of model in capturing depression-related signals across modalities, offering insights into effective multimodal representation strategies for mental health prediction.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided research paper. Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper explores and compares the effectiveness of three different types of models – XGBoost, transformer-based models, and large language models (LLMs) – for multimodal depression detection using the MPDD (Multimodal Personality-Aware Depression Detection) dataset. The study analyzes audio, video, and text features extracted from the dataset, assessing the strengths and limitations of each model in capturing depression-related signals across different modalities. The experiments are conducted on both elderly and younger participant datasets, with ablation studies to optimize model configurations and evaluation using weighted and unweighted F1 scores. The authors find that Transformer models tend to outperform XGBoost and LLMs on the given dataset, especially with shorter audio segments. They are the most effective for multimodal depression detection, but LLMs underperform relatively to transformers and even simpler models like XGBoost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. Applying XGBoost, Transformers, and LLMs to multimodal depression detection is not entirely new. Several existing studies have explored these model classes in this domain. The main novelty lies in: 1) its application to the *new* MPDD dataset and 2) the *direct comparison* of the performance of these three classes of models on the same dataset and tasks. The focus on both elderly and younger populations is a positive aspect, but that's not entirely novel either.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper hinges on the MPDD dataset and the insights gained from the comparative analysis.

    *   *MPDD Dataset:* If the MPDD dataset is significantly larger or more diverse than existing datasets, this gives the paper added importance. The paper highlights the diversity of this dataset.
    *   *Comparative Analysis:* The direct comparison of XGBoost, Transformers, and LLMs provides practical guidance for researchers and practitioners. It highlights that larger models (LLMs) do not always guarantee better performance on such datasets. It gives researchers information to choose appropriate model types given the computational cost/benefit profile.
    *   *Ablation Studies:* The ablation studies contribute to understanding the impact of specific techniques like PCA, class weighting, mixup augmentation, and cross-validation.
    *   *Model Optimization:* The configurations that are provided are beneficial.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Systematic Comparison:* The systematic comparison of multiple model types is valuable.
    *   *Ablation Studies:*  The rigorous ablation studies provide insights into the impact of various techniques.
    *   *Use of a novel dataset*

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Novelty in Methodology:* The core methods (XGBoost, Transformers, LLMs) are well-established. The adaptations for the MPDD dataset are not revolutionary.
    *   *Underperformance of LLMs:* The underperformance of LLMs is a key finding but may be a consequence of the dataset size and the relatively simple fine-tuning strategy employed. More sophisticated LLM training techniques might yield different results. Further exploration of different prompting strategies for the LLM could improve performance.
    *   *Lack of Theoretical Depth:* The paper is largely empirical, lacking a deep theoretical analysis of why each model performs differently.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper can serve as a useful benchmark for future research on multimodal depression detection using the MPDD dataset.
    *   The practical insights from the comparative analysis can guide model selection and optimization in real-world applications.
    *   The results may stimulate further research into more effective ways of leveraging LLMs for this task, especially given their potential for incorporating contextual knowledge and reasoning.

*   **Conclusion:** The paper contributes to the field by providing a comprehensive comparative analysis of different model types on the new MPDD dataset. While the methods themselves are not entirely novel, the insights gained from the ablation studies and the performance comparison are useful for researchers and practitioners working on multimodal depression detection. The finding that LLMs do not automatically outperform simpler models is also important.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The score of 6 reflects that the study provides a systematic empirical analysis that yields practically relevant insights from using the newly created MPDD dataset. However, there is limited theoretical depth and limited novelty in the methods themselves.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### cMALC-D: Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20818v1)
- **Authors**: Anirudh Satheesh, Keenan Powell, Hua Wei
- **Abstract**: Many multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) algorithms are trained in fixed simulation environments, making them brittle when deployed in real-world scenarios with more complex and uncertain conditions. Contextual MARL (cMARL) addresses this by parameterizing environments with context variables and training a context-agnostic policy that performs well across all environment configurations. Existing cMARL methods attempt to use curriculum learning to help train and evaluate context-agnostic policies, but they often rely on unreliable proxy signals, such as value estimates or generalized advantage estimates that are noisy and unstable in multi-agent settings due to inter-agent dynamics and partial observability. To address these issues, we propose Contextual Multi-Agent LLM-Guided Curriculum Learning with Diversity-Based Context Blending (cMALC-D), a framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate semantically meaningful curricula and provide a more robust evaluation signal. To prevent mode collapse and encourage exploration, we introduce a novel diversity-based context blending mechanism that creates new training scenarios by combining features from prior contexts. Experiments in traffic signal control domains demonstrate that cMALC-D significantly improves both generalization and sample efficiency compared to existing curriculum learning baselines. We provide code at https://github.com/DaRL-LibSignal/cMALC-D.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces cMALC-D, a novel framework for Contextual Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (CMARL) that utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate curricula for training MARL agents. The key innovations are: (1) using an LLM as a high-level controller to propose environment contexts based on the agent's learning progress, and (2) a diversity-based context blending mechanism to prevent mode collapse and encourage exploration of the context space.  Experiments conducted on traffic signal control environments demonstrate that cMALC-D improves generalization and sample efficiency compared to other curriculum learning baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper combines several ideas, including contextual MARL, LLM-guided curriculum learning, and diversity mechanisms. While LLMs have been used for RL curriculum generation before, applying them to *contextual multi-agent* settings and incorporating the diversity-based context blending is a novel contribution. The combination is non-trivial, as the multi-agent setting introduces challenges like non-stationarity and partial observability which the diversity mechanism seeks to mitigate.
* **Significance:** Generalization in MARL is a critical issue, and the paper addresses this directly. The application to traffic signal control is relevant and has real-world impact potential. Demonstrating improved generalization and sample efficiency are important results. The use of LLMs for curriculum generation could also lead to more automated and adaptive training procedures in MARL.
* **Strengths:**
    * The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of cMALC-D compared to strong baselines. The ablation studies further validate the contribution of the diversity mechanism.
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and results.
    * The choice of traffic signal control as an evaluation domain is relevant and allows for quantitative assessment of performance.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The dependency on LLMs can be a limitation. The computational cost of LLM inference can be high, especially for complex environments or scenarios requiring frequent curriculum updates. While the paper mitigates the cost via a quantized LLM and vLLM, the cost remains a potential bottleneck, especially as LLMs scale.
    * The context space is still relatively constrained.  While the LLM aids in curriculum generation, the actual parameters being manipulated are still predefined.  A truly open-ended exploration of context space might require more sophisticated techniques.
    * The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of *what* the LLM learns about the environment and the agent's performance.  While the correlation matrices provide some insight, more qualitative analysis would be useful.
    * The evaluation is limited to traffic control scenarios. Evaluating cMALC-D in other complex multi-agent environments (e.g., cooperative robotics, resource allocation) would strengthen the paper's generalizability claim.
* **Potential Impact:** The paper could influence future research in MARL, particularly in the area of curriculum learning. It demonstrates a promising approach for automating curriculum design using LLMs, paving the way for more adaptive and robust MARL systems. The idea of a diversity mechanism for context blending is also valuable and could be applied in other curriculum learning settings.  The paper also motivates future research directions to further explore incorporating the knowledge of LLMs on relationships between environment features.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper's approach leverages known components, the novel combination of LLM-guided curriculum generation, diversity-based context blending, and application to CMARL addresses an important problem with convincing results. The weaknesses, particularly the computational cost of LLMs and the constrained context space, limit its immediate practical impact. However, the paper demonstrates a solid step forward and opens up new research directions. The contribution to the field is notable and deserves recognition for promoting both generalization and automation.  The empirical results strongly justify this.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GDLLM: A Global Distance-aware Modeling Approach Based on Large Language Models for Event Temporal Relation Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20828v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Zhao, Wanting Ning, Yuxiao Fei, Yubo Feng, Lishuang Li
- **Abstract**: In Natural Language Processing(NLP), Event Temporal Relation Extraction (ETRE) is to recognize the temporal relations of two events. Prior studies have noted the importance of language models for ETRE. However, the restricted pre-trained knowledge of Small Language Models(SLMs) limits their capability to handle minority class relations in imbalanced classification datasets. For Large Language Models(LLMs), researchers adopt manually designed prompts or instructions, which may introduce extra noise, leading to interference with the model's judgment of the long-distance dependencies between events. To address these issues, we propose GDLLM, a Global Distance-aware modeling approach based on LLMs. We first present a distance-aware graph structure utilizing Graph Attention Network(GAT) to assist the LLMs in capturing long-distance dependency features. Additionally, we design a temporal feature learning paradigm based on soft inference to augment the identification of relations with a short-distance proximity band, which supplements the probabilistic information generated by LLMs into the multi-head attention mechanism. Since the global feature can be captured effectively, our framework substantially enhances the performance of minority relation classes and improves the overall learning ability. Experiments on two publicly available datasets, TB-Dense and MATRES, demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces GDLLM, a Global Distance-aware modeling approach for Event Temporal Relation Extraction (ETRE) that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs). The approach addresses limitations of Small Language Models (SLMs) in handling imbalanced data and the noisy nature of manually designed prompts often used with LLMs. GDLLM uses a distance-aware graph structure, incorporating a Graph Attention Network (GAT) to capture long-distance dependencies between events. Additionally, it introduces a temporal feature learning paradigm based on "soft inference," augmenting relation identification with short-distance proximity information. Experiments on TB-Dense and MATRES datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its combination of LLMs and GAT in a distance-aware manner *specifically designed for ETRE*.  The "soft inference" approach to temporal feature learning also adds a layer of novelty.  The paper convincingly argues that previous work either focuses on SLMs, which struggle with minority classes, or relies heavily on manual prompts with LLMs, leading to noise. GDLLM aims to bridge this gap. However, individual components like GATs and LLMs have been explored extensively; the innovation primarily resides in their integration and adaptation for ETRE's specific challenges.
*   **Significance:** The improved performance on two widely-used datasets is significant. The paper demonstrates an increased performance on minority relation classes, a known problem in ETRE. By addressing the issues of long-distance dependencies and imbalanced datasets, GDLLM potentially offers a more robust and practical solution compared to previous models. The ablation study further reinforces the importance of each component of GDLLM, reinforcing its design decisions. However, significance is slightly diminished due to the continued reliance on fine-tuning.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture with detailed explanations of each component.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on standard datasets.
    *   Ablation study demonstrating the contribution of each component.
    *   Addressing the limitations of existing approaches in ETRE.
    *   The code will be made publicly available.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core components (LLMs, GAT) are not novel in themselves.  The innovation is in the *application and integration* but this could be made even clearer in the writing.
    *   While the paper aims to address the limitations of manually designed prompts, it still relies on fine-tuning LLMs, thus inheriting some dependence on prompt engineering (albeit at a lower level). While the paper addresses zero-shot methods, the gains are not large enough.
    *   Limited discussion of computational cost and scalability, although this is a typical problem with ETRE research.
    *   Potential impact on other NLP fields could be highlighted further.
*   **Justification:** The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of ETRE. While the individual components are well-established, their integration within GDLLM effectively addresses specific challenges related to imbalanced data and long-distance dependencies. The comprehensive experimental results on two standard datasets and the extensive ablation study provide strong evidence for the model's effectiveness. Although the paper could improve on the degree of improvement over zero-shot approaches, the clear presentation, detailed architectural explanations, and thorough evaluation warrant a high score.
    **Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Publish to Perish: Prompt Injection Attacks on LLM-Assisted Peer Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20863v2)
- **Authors**: Matteo Gioele Collu, Umberto Salviati, Roberto Confalonieri, Mauro Conti, Giovanni Apruzzese
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into the scientific peer-review process, raising new questions about their reliability and resilience to manipulation. In this work, we investigate the potential for hidden prompt injection attacks, where authors embed adversarial text within a paper's PDF to influence the LLM-generated review. We begin by formalising three distinct threat models that envision attackers with different motivations -- not all of which implying malicious intent. For each threat model, we design adversarial prompts that remain invisible to human readers yet can steer an LLM's output toward the author's desired outcome. Using a user study with domain scholars, we derive four representative reviewing prompts used to elicit peer reviews from LLMs. We then evaluate the robustness of our adversarial prompts across (i) different reviewing prompts, (ii) different commercial LLM-based systems, and (iii) different peer-reviewed papers. Our results show that adversarial prompts can reliably mislead the LLM, sometimes in ways that adversely affect a "honest-but-lazy" reviewer. Finally, we propose and empirically assess methods to reduce detectability of adversarial prompts under automated content checks.
- **Summary**: Here's a breakdown of the prompt's key elements:

*   **Emphasis on critical evaluation:** The prompt uses strong language like "rigorous and critical evaluation" and "critical" to underscore the need for a detailed and skeptical review.
*   **Clear and objective reasoning:** The prompt asks for "clear, well-reasoned arguments" to justify the assigned score, encouraging a thorough explanation.
*   **Justification of score:** It requests a "thorough justification," emphasizing that the reviewer should explain the paper's strengths and weaknesses and their impact on the field.
*   **Novelty and significance:** The reviewer is told to focus on the novelty and significance of the paper.
*   **Explicit formatting:** It instructs the reviewer to include the score in a specific format ('Score: X'), signaling the importance of this element.

### Deep Learning Framework for Early Detection of Pancreatic Cancer Using Multi-Modal Medical Imaging Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20877v1)
- **Authors**: Dennis Slobodzian, Karissa Tilbury, Amir Kordijazi
- **Abstract**: Pacreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) remains one of the most lethal forms of cancer, with a five-year survival rate below 10% primarily due to late detection. This research develops and validates a deep learning framework for early PDAC detection through analysis of dual-modality imaging: autofluorescence and second harmonic generation (SHG). We analyzed 40 unique patient samples to create a specialized neural network capable of distinguishing between normal, fibrotic, and cancerous tissue. Our methodology evaluated six distinct deep learning architectures, comparing traditional Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) with modern Vision Transformers (ViTs). Through systematic experimentation, we identified and overcome significant challenges in medical image analysis, including limited dataset size and class imbalance. The final optimized framework, based on a modified ResNet architecture with frozen pre-trained layers and class-weighted training, achieved over 90% accuracy in cancer detection. This represents a significant improvement over current manual analysis methods an demonstrates potential for clinical deployment. This work establishes a robust pipeline for automated PDAC detection that can augment pathologists' capabilities while providing a foundation for future expansion to other cancer types. The developed methodology also offers valuable insights for applying deep learning to limited-size medical imaging datasets, a common challenge in clinical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a deep learning framework for early detection of pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) using dual-modality imaging (autofluorescence and second harmonic generation - SHG). The authors developed a specialized neural network capable of distinguishing between normal, fibrotic, and cancerous pancreatic tissue. They experimented with different deep learning architectures (CNNs and Vision Transformers), addressing challenges like limited dataset size and class imbalance using techniques such as frozen pre-trained layers and class-weighted training. The final optimized framework, based on a modified ResNet architecture, achieved over 90% accuracy in cancer detection. The paper emphasizes the framework's potential to augment pathologists' capabilities and its adaptability to other cancer types and limited-size medical imaging datasets. The work includes a model interpretability analysis using Grad-CAM, offering transparency into the model's decision-making process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** Early detection of PDAC is crucial for improving survival rates.
    *   **Novel dual-modality approach:** Uses autofluorescence and SHG imaging, which isn't a common combination in existing PDAC detection DL models.
    *   **Systematic experimentation:** Thoroughly evaluates multiple deep learning architectures and optimization techniques.
    *   **Focus on practical challenges:** Addresses limitations such as limited dataset size, class imbalance, and overfitting, issues common in medical imaging.
    *   **High accuracy results:** Achieves over 90% accuracy, which is a significant improvement over manual analysis and some existing methods.
    *   **Interpretability analysis:** Includes Grad-CAM visualization, making the model more transparent and potentially increasing clinician trust.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Offers a public Github repository with the code and integration scripts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** While the study addresses this challenge, the small dataset (40 patients, 239 images) raises questions about the generalizability of the results to larger, more diverse populations.
    *   **Single-center data:** The data originates from a single institution (Maine Medical Center BioBank), which could introduce bias and limit external validity.
    *   **Lack of Prospective Validation:** The study is retrospective. Prospective validation in a clinical setting is needed to confirm the model's effectiveness in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Relatively Simple Architectures:** While the ResNet-18 worked best, the evaluation was only over ResNet-18, ResNet-34 and ResNet-50 architectures. It may be beneficial to evaluate other modern architectures.
    *   **Normal/Fibrosis issue:** While the binary classification model solves this problem, the initial analysis reveals normal and fibrosis feature class discrimination problems which can be investigated further.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the specific application of deep learning to combined autofluorescence and SHG imaging for PDAC detection, the systematic exploration of architectural and training strategies to address dataset limitations, and the inclusion of interpretability analysis. While individual components (deep learning for cancer detection, SHG imaging, transfer learning) are not entirely new, the combination of these elements to address a specific problem in PDAC diagnosis represents a valuable contribution.

The significance of the paper is moderate to high. The methodology provides a framework for addressing common challenges in medical image analysis with limited data and the achieved accuracy is promising for clinical translation as an assistive technology. The interpretability analysis enhances the practical utility of the method. However, the limitations associated with the dataset (size, single center) need to be addressed before widespread adoption. The work can serve as a foundation for future research, especially with more comprehensive and diverse datasets.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a well-executed study with promising results for PDAC detection using an innovative dual-modality imaging approach. It addresses key challenges in medical image analysis, particularly those related to limited data and class imbalance, using systematic experimentation. The inclusion of model interpretability enhances the clinical relevance of the work. While the small dataset size and single-center origin limit the generalizability of the findings, the paper lays a solid foundation for future research and clinical application, hence the following score:

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Understanding and evaluating computer vision models through the lens of counterfactuals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20881v1)
- **Authors**: Pushkar Shukla
- **Abstract**: Counterfactual reasoning -- the practice of asking ``what if'' by varying inputs and observing changes in model behavior -- has become central to interpretable and fair AI. This thesis develops frameworks that use counterfactuals to explain, audit, and mitigate bias in vision classifiers and generative models. By systematically altering semantically meaningful attributes while holding others fixed, these methods uncover spurious correlations, probe causal dependencies, and help build more robust systems. The first part addresses vision classifiers. CAVLI integrates attribution (LIME) with concept-level analysis (TCAV) to quantify how strongly decisions rely on human-interpretable concepts. With localized heatmaps and a Concept Dependency Score, CAVLI shows when models depend on irrelevant cues like backgrounds. Extending this, ASAC introduces adversarial counterfactuals that perturb protected attributes while preserving semantics. Through curriculum learning, ASAC fine-tunes biased models for improved fairness and accuracy while avoiding stereotype-laden artifacts. The second part targets generative Text-to-Image (TTI) models. TIBET provides a scalable pipeline for evaluating prompt-sensitive biases by varying identity-related terms, enabling causal auditing of how race, gender, and age affect image generation. To capture interactions, BiasConnect builds causal graphs diagnosing intersectional biases. Finally, InterMit offers a modular, training-free algorithm that mitigates intersectional bias via causal sensitivity scores and user-defined fairness goals. Together, these contributions show counterfactuals as a unifying lens for interpretability, fairness, and causality in both discriminative and generative models, establishing principled, scalable methods for socially responsible bias evaluation and mitigation.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the thesis, along with a novelty and significance score:

**Summary**

The thesis "Understanding and evaluating computer vision models through the lens of counterfactuals" by Pushkar Shukla addresses a critical need for explainable and fair AI by developing counterfactual-based methods for understanding and mitigating biases in computer vision and text-to-image models. The thesis introduces several novel frameworks: CAVLI, which quantifies concept influence on classifier decisions; ASACs, which mitigates bias through adversarial counterfactuals; TIBET, which evaluates biases in text-to-image generative models; BiasConnect, which measures intersectional effects between social attributes; and InterMit, a modular algorithm for intersectional bias mitigation. The methods aim to uncover spurious correlations, interrogate causal dependencies, and build more robust and fair models. The work provides novel approaches for model interpretation, bias mitigation, and intersectionality analysis in both discriminative and generative contexts.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Strengths:*

*   **Novelty:** The thesis demonstrates substantial novelty in several key areas. The CAVLI approach provides a unique way to connect concept activation and LIME explanations. ASACs offer a new technique to generate counterfactuals while preserving realism and avoiding common artifacts in generative methods. TIBET and BiasConnect provide novel approaches for understanding and mitigating biases in text-to-image models, with a particular focus on intersectionality. InterMit provides a novel algorithm for mitigating intersectional biases. The combination of these components into a cohesive counterfactual reasoning framework is a significant contribution.
*   **Relevance:** The thesis tackles highly relevant and timely problems in computer vision and AI: explainability, fairness, and the mitigation of biases. The increasing deployment of AI systems in socially sensitive contexts requires methods to ensure transparency, accountability, and fairness.
*   **Practicality:** The proposed methods are not just theoretical; they are evaluated on real-world datasets and models. Moreover, the modular design of InterMit allows for practical deployment, and the diagnostic components (CAVLI, TIBET, BiasConnect) provide actionable insights for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Thoroughness:** The thesis provides a comprehensive background on counterfactual reasoning, fairness, and explainability. The experimental results are thorough and include both quantitative and qualitative evaluations, ablation studies, and user studies.
*   **Contribution to Generative Models:** It addresses a relative gap in research on how to effectively understand and address fairness in generative models as compared to classification ones. This is significant given the increasing power and prevalence of such models.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Reliance on Existing Components:** While the thesis combines existing techniques (e.g., LIME, TCAV) in novel ways, the core components themselves are not entirely original. However, the creativity lies in how they are combined and adapted.
*   **Scalability and Automation:** Some components, like concept selection in CAVLI or prompt generation in TIBET, rely on human input or pre-defined knowledge bases, potentially limiting their scalability and automation. But the new research focuses on the AI approach and the results have merit without full automation.
*   **Evaluation of InterMit and Intersectional Measures:** The evaluation relies on a specific set of models, prompting strategies and data that are used for demonstration. The generalizability to various different AI tools and platforms could use additional validation or consideration.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** While the thesis acknowledges ethical considerations, the discussion could be more in-depth, exploring potential misuse scenarios or unintended consequences in more detail.
*   **Complexity**: The comprehensive toolsets may be challenging for new users to use and navigate effectively, and additional guidance or explanations of the toolkits might be valuable.

**Novelty and Significance Score:** 8/10

**Rationale:**

The thesis makes a substantial contribution to the field of computer vision and AI safety, particularly in addressing fairness and explainability challenges. It introduces several novel frameworks that build upon and extend existing methods, offering practical solutions for understanding and mitigating biases in both classification and generative models. The emphasis on intersectionality in generative models is particularly significant, tackling a previously underexplored area. The rigorous evaluation and the modular design of InterMit are further strengths. The limitations related to scalability and reliance on existing components are acknowledged, which temper the novelty somewhat, although the results presented with the AI approach and the components that are used demonstrate a significant advance that contributes toward fairness and equity in AI. Overall, the thesis demonstrates a deep understanding of the problems it addresses and proposes well-reasoned and potentially impactful solutions.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Lattice Random Walk Discretisations of Stochastic Differential Equations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20883v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Duffield, Maxwell Aifer, Denis Melanson, Zach Belateche, Patrick J. Coles
- **Abstract**: We introduce a lattice random walk discretisation scheme for stochastic differential equations (SDEs) that samples binary or ternary increments at each step, suppressing complex drift and diffusion computations to simple 1 or 2 bit random values. This approach is a significant departure from traditional floating point discretisations and offers several advantages; including compatibility with stochastic computing architectures that avoid floating-point arithmetic in place of directly manipulating the underlying probability distribution of a bitstream, elimination of Gaussian sampling requirements, robustness to quantisation errors, and handling of non-Lipschitz drifts. We prove weak convergence and demonstrate the advantages through experiments on various SDEs, including state-of-the-art diffusion models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel lattice random walk (LRW) discretization scheme for stochastic differential equations (SDEs).  This scheme replaces complex drift and diffusion computations with simpler binary or ternary random increments. Key advantages claimed include compatibility with stochastic computing architectures, elimination of Gaussian sampling requirements, robustness to quantization errors, and improved handling of non-Lipschitz drift functions. The paper proves weak convergence of the LRW scheme and presents experimental results on various SDEs, including state-of-the-art diffusion models, demonstrating its advantages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of the LRW discretization is reasonably novel. While discretizing SDEs is a well-established area, the authors’ approach of simplifying computations to bit-level operations using a lattice random walk is a departure from traditional floating-point based schemes and draws a parallel with Stochastic Computing architectures.  The concept of ternary (or binary) increments is not entirely new in SDE discretization, but their use in *fully* absorbing drift and diffusion calculations into the random sampling step (rather than just diffusion), enabling direct compatibility with Stochastic Computing architectures, distinguishes the LRW scheme.

*   **Significance:**  The potential significance lies primarily in two areas:
    *   **Stochastic Computing:** If the LRW scheme can genuinely unlock the potential of stochastic computing for SDE simulation, this could lead to substantial speedups and energy efficiency gains. This is especially relevant for computationally intensive applications like diffusion models and molecular dynamics.
    *   **Robustness:**  The claimed robustness to quantization error is a significant advantage, particularly in the context of deploying SDE-based models on resource-constrained hardware. The ability to handle non-Lipschitz drifts is also crucial for certain applications where traditional methods may fail.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing SDE discretization methods and motivates the need for a more hardware-friendly and robust alternative.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** The proof of weak convergence provides a theoretical foundation for the LRW scheme.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experimental results, especially on the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and Stable Diffusion models, provide initial evidence of the advantages of LRW. The demonstration of robustness to quantization is particularly compelling.
    *   **Connections to Relevant Fields:** The paper bridges SDE discretization with Stochastic Computing, a promising avenue for future hardware acceleration.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Diagonal Diffusion Assumption:** The limitation to diagonal diffusion matrices is a significant restriction. While the authors suggest the Lamperti transform as a workaround for some cases, this adds complexity and may not be applicable to all SDEs. This drastically limits the generality of their method.
    *   **Limited Comparison:**  The experiments focus primarily on comparisons with Euler-Maruyama, even though other methods (as listed in Table I) are arguably more sophisticated and might provide a more challenging baseline. The comparison with BAOAB-limit schemes would be of interest, especially given their relevance to molecular dynamics.
    *   **Lack of Stochastic Computing Implementation:** While the paper claims stochastic computing compatibility, it lacks any concrete implementation or simulation results demonstrating the actual performance benefits on stochastic computing hardware. The speedup and energy efficiency claims remain largely speculative.
    *   **Weak Convergence Only:** Proving strong convergence would significantly strengthen the theoretical foundation of the work, which could give more confidence that the solution path closely matches the true SDE solution.
    *   **Choice of Delta:** The spatial step size can be seen as a tuning parameter, which might need careful parameter tuning or automatic tuning. It's not really a limitation but is still another parameter that needs to be specified.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the LRW scheme can be successfully implemented and optimized on stochastic computing hardware, it could have a significant impact on the field of SDE simulation, enabling new possibilities for large-scale modeling and analysis. The improved robustness and handling of non-Lipschitz drifts could also make it a valuable tool for certain applications.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant discretization scheme with a promising connection to emerging hardware architectures. However, the significant limitations (diagonal diffusion matrices, lack of stochastic computing implementation, weak convergence only), and the relatively limited experimental validation temper enthusiasm. While the theoretical foundation and initial experimental results are encouraging, more extensive research is needed to fully realize its potential. Therefore, the score has been carefully considered based on these factors.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: math.NA
- **Score**: 6/10

### PromptSleuth: Detecting Prompt Injection via Semantic Intent Invariance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20890v1)
- **Authors**: Mengxiao Wang, Yuxuan Zhang, Guofei Gu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into real-world applications, from virtual assistants to autonomous agents. However, their flexibility also introduces new attack vectors-particularly Prompt Injection (PI), where adversaries manipulate model behavior through crafted inputs. As attackers continuously evolve with paraphrased, obfuscated, and even multi-task injection strategies, existing benchmarks are no longer sufficient to capture the full spectrum of emerging threats. To address this gap, we construct a new benchmark that systematically extends prior efforts. Our benchmark subsumes the two widely-used existing ones while introducing new manipulation techniques and multi-task scenarios, thereby providing a more comprehensive evaluation setting. We find that existing defenses, though effective on their original benchmarks, show clear weaknesses under our benchmark, underscoring the need for more robust solutions. Our key insight is that while attack forms may vary, the adversary's intent-injecting an unauthorized task-remains invariant. Building on this observation, we propose PromptSleuth, a semantic-oriented defense framework that detects prompt injection by reasoning over task-level intent rather than surface features. Evaluated across state-of-the-art benchmarks, PromptSleuth consistently outperforms existing defense while maintaining comparable runtime and cost efficiency. These results demonstrate that intent-based semantic reasoning offers a robust, efficient, and generalizable strategy for defending LLMs against evolving prompt injection threats.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "PromptSleuth: Detecting Prompt Injection via Semantic Intent Invariance" addresses the increasing threat of prompt injection attacks in Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that existing defenses are brittle, focusing on syntactic patterns that attackers can easily circumvent. They propose a novel approach, PromptSleuth, which focuses on semantic intent invariance: detecting when a user prompt attempts to inject a task that's semantically inconsistent with the system's intended function.  The authors construct a new benchmark, PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH, which expands on existing datasets by including new attack techniques (context tampering, instruction wrapping, payload splitting, emotional manipulation, reward framing, and threat-based coercion) and multi-task adversarial scenarios. PromptSleuth works by summarizing the system prompt and user prompt into abstract tasks, generating a task-relationship graph to identify related and unrelated tasks, and using clustering to isolate injected tasks. Experiments show that PromptSleuth outperforms existing defenses while maintaining efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core idea of focusing on semantic intent rather than syntactic patterns is a valuable contribution. While other defenses have used semantics to some degree, PromptSleuth's systematic approach to intent extraction, relationship analysis, and clustering is novel. The PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH benchmark is also a significant contribution. The creation of such a benchmark, which exposes the weaknesses of existing defenses, justifies the need for more robust methods and provides a standardized way to evaluate future defenses. The introduction of novel attack techniques such as context tampering and manipulation strategies also boosts the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** Prompt injection attacks are a critical security concern for LLMs. The paper’s identification of limitations in current defense strategies and the proposal of PromptSleuth address a gap in the field. The improved performance on a broader range of attacks suggests that PromptSleuth has the potential to be more robust and generalizable than existing solutions. The modular architecture and adaptability with various LLM backends further increase its practical significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental results.
    *   The PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH benchmark is a valuable resource for the research community.
    *   The emphasis on semantic reasoning provides a more principled approach to defense compared to pattern matching.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of PromptSleuth against a wider range of attacks.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the design choices and trade-offs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance of PromptSleuth is dependent on the LLM used for summarization and relationship analysis. A limitation is that smaller LLMs may not be able to reliably identify complex injections. The effectiveness of the approach hinges on the abilities of the detector LLM.
    *   The experimental results could be strengthened by including a wider range of baselines, particularly those that also incorporate semantic analysis.
    *   The overhead introduced by PromptSleuth needs to be further optimized for real-time deployments, especially for larger models and more complex prompts.
    *   Further details concerning the prompts utilized to create both the benchmark and the proposed PromptSleuth defence would benefit the replicability.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper is likely to influence future research in prompt injection defenses, encouraging a shift from syntactic to semantic approaches.
    *   The PROMPTSLEUTH-BENCH benchmark will likely become a standard tool for evaluating new defenses.
    *   The modular architecture of PromptSleuth could inspire the development of more flexible and adaptable defense systems.
    *   The success of the proposed system indicates that intent-based semantic reasoning is a more reliable mechanism for defence.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of prompt injection defenses by focusing on semantic intent invariance. The introduction of a new benchmark and a demonstrably effective defense system, PromptSleuth, validates the approach. The paper is not without its weaknesses, primarily related to its reliance on large LLMs and the need for further optimization, and more baselines could have strengthened the paper, but its potential impact on the field is significant.
