### ELV-Halluc: Benchmarking Semantic Aggregation Hallucinations in Long Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21496v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Lu, Jiahao Wang, Yaolun Zhang, Ruohui Wang, Xuanyu Zheng, Yepeng Tang, Dahua Lin, Lewei Lu
- **Abstract**: Video multimodal large language models (Video-MLLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in video understanding. However, they remain vulnerable to hallucination-producing content inconsistent with or unrelated to video inputs. Previous video hallucination benchmarks primarily focus on short-videos. They attribute hallucinations to factors such as strong language priors, missing frames, or vision-language biases introduced by the visual encoder. While these causes indeed account for most hallucinations in short videos, they still oversimplify the cause of hallucinations. Sometimes, models generate incorrect outputs but with correct frame-level semantics. We refer to this type of hallucination as Semantic Aggregation Hallucination (SAH), which arises during the process of aggregating frame-level semantics into event-level semantic groups. Given that SAH becomes particularly critical in long videos due to increased semantic complexity across multiple events, it is essential to separate and thoroughly investigate the causes of this type of hallucination. To address the above issues, we introduce ELV-Halluc, the first benchmark dedicated to long-video hallucination, enabling a systematic investigation of SAH. Our experiments confirm the existence of SAH and show that it increases with semantic complexity. Additionally, we find that models are more prone to SAH on rapidly changing semantics. Moreover, we discuss potential approaches to mitigate SAH. We demonstrate that positional encoding strategy contributes to alleviating SAH, and further adopt DPO strategy to enhance the model's ability to distinguish semantics within and across events. To support this, we curate a dataset of 8K adversarial data pairs and achieve improvements on both ELV-Halluc and Video-MME, including a substantial 27.7% reduction in SAH ratio.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ELV-Halluc: Benchmarking Semantic Aggregation Hallucinations in Long Video Understanding":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of "Semantic Aggregation Hallucination" (SAH) in video multimodal large language models (Video-MLLMs), particularly in long videos. SAH occurs when a model correctly identifies frame-level semantics but incorrectly aggregates them across different events, leading to inaccurate event-level understanding. To address this, the authors introduce ELV-Halluc, a new benchmark specifically designed for long-video hallucination, focusing on SAH. They conduct experiments that demonstrate SAH's correlation with semantic complexity and rapid semantic changes. The paper also explores mitigation strategies, including improved positional encodings and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), achieving significant reductions in SAH ratio and improved overall performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in identifying and characterizing SAH as a distinct type of hallucination in long videos. While previous work has touched upon hallucination in Video-MLLMs, the explicit focus on aggregation errors and the introduction of a targeted benchmark are significant contributions. The triplet-based QA design and the SAH ratio metric offer a systematic way to measure this phenomenon.

*   **Significance:** Addressing SAH is crucial for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of Video-MLLMs, especially as they are applied to longer, more complex video content. The ELV-Halluc benchmark provides a valuable resource for the community to study and mitigate this type of error. The explored mitigation strategies provide a starting point for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of SAH and its importance.
    *   **Targeted Benchmark:** ELV-Halluc is well-designed to specifically evaluate SAH.
    *   **Thorough Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple models and different factors affecting SAH.
    *   **Demonstrated Mitigation Strategies:** The effectiveness of positional encoding and DPO in reducing SAH is convincingly shown.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Gemini Bias:** The use of Gemini-generated captions for initial annotations may introduce bias, as the authors acknowledge, and this limits the objectivity of comparing models to Gemini.
    *   **Limited Dataset Scale:** The dataset size is relatively small. While understandable given the manual annotation effort, a larger dataset could provide more robust and generalizable results.
    *   **Realism of the Dataset:** The event-based video construction, while helpful for control, might deviate from real-world long-video characteristics.
    *   **Simplification of mitigation strategies:** While the proposed solutions (positional encoding & DPO) show promise, they are likely not a complete solution to SAH and could be elaborated upon.

*   **Potential Influence:** ELV-Halluc is likely to become a standard benchmark for evaluating SAH in Video-MLLMs. The insights gained from this work will guide future research on improving the aggregation capabilities of these models and making them more reliable for long-video understanding tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper presents a novel problem, a well-designed benchmark, and compelling experimental results. The limitations (Gemini bias, dataset size) are acknowledged and do not significantly detract from the overall contribution. The potential influence of this work on the field is significant, as it highlights a critical issue and provides a valuable tool for addressing it. While the proposed solutions are not complete fixes for SAH, they give some insight on the problem at hand. This work is a significant step toward more reliable long-video understanding.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Spiking Decision Transformers: Local Plasticity, Phase-Coding, and Dendritic Routing for Low-Power Sequence Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21505v1)
- **Authors**: Vishal Pandey, Debasmita Biswas
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning agents based on Transformer architectures have achieved impressive performance on sequential decision-making tasks, but their reliance on dense matrix operations makes them ill-suited for energy-constrained, edge-oriented platforms. Spiking neural networks promise ultra-low-power, event-driven inference, yet no prior work has seamlessly merged spiking dynamics with return-conditioned sequence modeling. We present the Spiking Decision Transformer (SNN-DT), which embeds Leaky Integrate-and-Fire neurons into each self-attention block, trains end-to-end via surrogate gradients, and incorporates biologically inspired three-factor plasticity, phase-shifted spike-based positional encodings, and a lightweight dendritic routing module. Our implementation matches or exceeds standard Decision Transformer performance on classic control benchmarks (CartPole-v1, MountainCar-v0, Acrobot-v1, Pendulum-v1) while emitting fewer than ten spikes per decision, an energy proxy suggesting over four orders-of-magnitude reduction in per inference energy. By marrying sequence modeling with neuromorphic efficiency, SNN-DT opens a pathway toward real-time, low-power control on embedded and wearable devices.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary, a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and a score with a robust justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Spiking Decision Transformer (SNN-DT), a novel architecture that combines spiking neural networks (SNNs) with the Decision Transformer (DT) framework for reinforcement learning. This aims to create energy-efficient, bio-plausible RL agents suitable for edge computing. SNN-DT incorporates three key neuromorphic innovations: 1) a three-factor plasticity rule in the action head for local learning, 2) phase-shifted spike-based positional encodings for temporal representation, and 3) a dendritic-style routing module to dynamically weight attention heads. The authors demonstrate that SNN-DT achieves comparable or better performance than its non-spiking counterpart on classic control benchmarks while drastically reducing the number of spikes per decision, thus indicating a significant reduction in energy consumption. The paper focuses on offline RL, where the policy is learned from a fixed dataset of trajectories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel combination of SNNs and the DT architecture. While SNNs and Transformers have been explored separately, their seamless integration for return-conditioned sequence modeling in RL is a significant contribution. The specific neuromorphic adaptations, particularly the three-factor plasticity, phase-shifted spike encoding, and dendritic routing, are well-motivated and contribute to the bio-plausibility and energy efficiency of the model. The architecture's novelty also lies in the fact that prior work does not address decision-making tasks at the scale of return-conditioned policies.

    *   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical limitation of standard Transformers in RL: their energy inefficiency, which hinders their deployment on edge devices. The demonstrated reduction in spike activity suggests a substantial potential for energy savings, making the DT architecture more practical for real-world applications like robotics, drones, and wearables. By linking spiking networks with temporal representations, routing, and the plasticity mechanisms required for real-world control, this work bridges the gap between the brain's computational efficiency and the power of transformer models.

    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental validation across several classic control benchmarks. The ablation studies are particularly valuable, as they clearly demonstrate the individual contributions of each neuromorphic module. The inclusion of metrics like spike counts and CPU latency provides a solid basis for assessing the energy efficiency and real-time performance of the model. The evaluation includes several ablations, and also the hyperparameter tuning/sweep that provides an overall performance.

    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the SNN-DT architecture, its components, and the experimental setup. Figures and tables are used effectively to illustrate the key concepts and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Energy Proxy:** The use of spike count as an energy proxy is a reasonable approximation, but it would be strengthened by actual hardware measurements. Simulation doesn't account for factors like memory accesses and power consumption which may vary depending on deployment substrate (Loihi 2 vs TrueNorth for example). The paper mentions such limitations in the discussion. Actual power measurements on neuromorphic hardware would significantly bolster the claims of energy efficiency.

    *   **Scope of Benchmarks:** The experiments are limited to classic control benchmarks. While these are standard, they may not fully capture the complexities of more realistic RL tasks. The performance of SNN-DT on more complex, high-dimensional environments should be explored. It needs more real-world tests.

    *   **Online Learning:** The paper focuses primarily on offline RL. While the three-factor plasticity rule suggests potential for online learning, this is not fully explored experimentally. Demonstrating the online adaptation capabilities of SNN-DT would further enhance its practical relevance.

    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** While the empirical results are compelling, a more in-depth theoretical analysis of the convergence properties and generalization capabilities of the SNN-DT would be valuable. A theoretical understanding of how the three-factor plasticity interacts with the offline RL objective could also be beneficial.

*   **Overall Impression:**

This is a strong paper that makes a significant contribution to the intersection of neuromorphic computing and reinforcement learning. The novel architecture, thorough experimental validation, and clear presentation make it a valuable addition to the field. While some limitations exist, they point to promising avenues for future research.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

I assign a score of 8 because the paper demonstrates significant novelty, a clear path toward impactful applications, and rigorous experimental validation. However, it is not a 9 or 10 because the energy efficiency claims are based on a proxy metric rather than actual hardware measurements, the benchmark tasks are relatively simple, and further exploration of online learning capabilities and theoretical underpinnings is warranted. Nonetheless, this is a very promising work with the potential to significantly influence the development of energy-efficient RL agents.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Accept or Deny? Evaluating LLM Fairness and Performance in Loan Approval across Table-to-Text Serialization Approaches
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21512v1)
- **Authors**: Israel Abebe Azime, Deborah D. Kanubala, Tejumade Afonja, Mario Fritz, Isabel Valera, Dietrich Klakow, Philipp Slusallek
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly employed in high-stakes decision-making tasks, such as loan approvals. While their applications expand across domains, LLMs struggle to process tabular data, ensuring fairness and delivering reliable predictions. In this work, we assess the performance and fairness of LLMs on serialized loan approval datasets from three geographically distinct regions: Ghana, Germany, and the United States. Our evaluation focuses on the model's zero-shot and in-context learning (ICL) capabilities. Our results reveal that the choice of serialization (Serialization refers to the process of converting tabular data into text formats suitable for processing by LLMs.) format significantly affects both performance and fairness in LLMs, with certain formats such as GReat and LIFT yielding higher F1 scores but exacerbating fairness disparities. Notably, while ICL improved model performance by 4.9-59.6% relative to zero-shot baselines, its effect on fairness varied considerably across datasets. Our work underscores the importance of effective tabular data representation methods and fairness-aware models to improve the reliability of LLMs in financial decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance and fairness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of loan approval, focusing on how different serialization methods and in-context learning (ICL) impact outcomes. The authors evaluate LLMs on loan approval datasets from three geographically diverse regions (Ghana, Germany, and the United States).  The study explores the effects of various serialization formats (JSON, Text, GReaT, LIFT, etc.) and in-context learning (ICL) on both the accuracy and fairness metrics (Statistical Parity and Equality of Opportunity). The research explores different LLMs, including those fine-tuned for financial tasks and general-purpose models, assessing their performance in zero-shot and few-shot learning scenarios. The authors also delve into token attribution as a means of understanding model decision-making.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive assessment of LLMs in a high-stakes financial task (loan approval) across diverse geographical contexts and serialization methods.  Prior work has explored LLMs for financial tasks, but this paper's focus on fairness *across* different serialization formats and *geographical* datasets brings a new perspective. The comparison of domain-specific and general-purpose models is valuable, and the exploration of ICL's impact on both performance and fairness adds to the novelty. The investigation of different geographical datasets adds a valuable perspective on how LLMs manage data diversity.

*   **Significance:**  The paper highlights a crucial issue: LLMs, despite their increasing use in critical decision-making, can exhibit biases and inconsistencies when handling structured data like loan applications. Demonstrating that the choice of serialization format significantly influences both performance *and* fairness is a significant finding with practical implications. The finding that ICL doesn't consistently improve fairness, and can even worsen it, is equally important.  The research underscores the need for careful consideration of data representation and fairness-aware models when deploying LLMs in financial contexts. By investigating fairness by conducting few-shot experiments, the study highlights that few-shot examples can introduce significant disparities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a thorough benchmark evaluation, considering several LLMs, serialization formats, and datasets.
    *   **Focus on Fairness:** Explicitly evaluating fairness metrics and considering their interplay with performance is a key strength.
    *   **Geographical Diversity:** Using datasets from Ghana, Germany, and the US provides valuable insights into cross-cultural applicability and potential biases.
    *   **Analysis of ICL:** The exploration of ICL's effectiveness and its impact on fairness offers actionable insights.
    *   **Token Attribution:** The token attribution analysis, while having some limitations, is a worthwhile attempt to understand model decision-making.
    *   **Well Structured Paper:** The research questions are well defined, and the methodology is clear.
    *   **Acknowledged Limitations:** The authors acknowledge limitations, such as the number of datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Baseline Performance:** The fact that LLMs often underperform a simple Logistic Regression model in zero-shot settings is concerning and raises questions about the overall suitability of *current* LLMs for this specific task without more extensive fine-tuning/prompt engineering.  This somewhat tempers the impact of the fairness analysis, as the models aren't performing well to begin with.
    *   **Token Attribution Limitations:** Token attribution results are inconclusive and hard to draw solid conclusion from. This limits the ability to understand exactly *why* fairness disparities arise. This leads to a more speculative discussion.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** Prompt engineering is not the core focus of this paper.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Tabular Foundation Models:** Though acknowledged, the lack of comparison to Tabular Foundation Models could provide a strong point of reference.
    *   **Reliance on pre-defined datasets**: While the use of pre-existing datasets provide a great overview, more diversity and further data points would be welcome.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings can influence practitioners in the financial industry to be cautious about deploying LLMs for high-stakes decisions like loan approval without careful consideration of data representation, fairness, and robust evaluation. It can motivate further research into fairness-aware LLMs, serialization methods that minimize bias, and methods for explaining LLM decision-making in structured data contexts. The inclusion of token attributions experiments contribute valuable insights to the field.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper clearly frames the research question, the methodology, and findings. The authors correctly point out the limitations and potential biases that would have otherwise clouded the results. For example, they are extremely clear about the prompt engineering process, and the usage of "LLMS that we, to the best of our knowledge, would be adapted to several use cases because of popularity, open source and con- tinued support by organizations that release them.".

**Score: 7.5**

The paper offers a significant and novel investigation into the complexities of using LLMs for loan approval, with a strong emphasis on fairness. The comprehensive evaluation across diverse datasets and serialization methods is a strength. The limitations, particularly regarding the suboptimal zero-shot performance relative to a classical baseline and the inconclusive token attribution, slightly temper the overall impact. The inclusion of token attributions and the explanation of limitations contribute a valuable contribution for the field, particularly due to the strong reasoning throughout the article. The findings highlight key challenges and offer actionable insights for practitioners and researchers, solidifying its contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Maybe you don't need a U-Net: convolutional feature upsampling for materials micrograph segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21529v1)
- **Authors**: Ronan Docherty, Antonis Vamvakeros, Samuel J. Cooper
- **Abstract**: Feature foundation models - usually vision transformers - offer rich semantic descriptors of images, useful for downstream tasks such as (interactive) segmentation and object detection. For computational efficiency these descriptors are often patch-based, and so struggle to represent the fine features often present in micrographs; they also struggle with the large image sizes present in materials and biological image analysis. In this work, we train a convolutional neural network to upsample low-resolution (i.e, large patch size) foundation model features with reference to the input image. We apply this upsampler network (without any further training) to efficiently featurise and then segment a variety of microscopy images, including plant cells, a lithium-ion battery cathode and organic crystals. The richness of these upsampled features admits separation of hard to segment phases, like hairline cracks. We demonstrate that interactive segmentation with these deep features produces high-quality segmentations far faster and with far fewer labels than training or finetuning a more traditional convolutional network.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a convolutional feature upsampling method for segmenting materials science micrographs.  It leverages the rich semantic information encoded in features extracted from vision transformers (ViTs), specifically DINOv2, but addresses their coarse resolution. The method trains a convolutional neural network (CNN) to upsample these low-resolution ViT features with guidance from the input image.  The upsampled features are then combined with traditional, hand-engineered features for interactive segmentation, achieving faster and more accurate results with less labeled data than traditional CNN-based approaches or direct fine-tuning of ViTs. The authors demonstrate the approach on various microscopy datasets and benchmark datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of combining foundation model features with classical features is not entirely new (see citations in the paper). The novelty here lies primarily in the efficient convolutional upsampling strategy. This avoids the computational burdens of other upsampling methods (e.g., strided convolutions with overlapping patches or per-image optimization) and the complexities of methods which alter attention mechanisms. Using the *image* to guide the convolutional upsampling network seems like a reasonable choice, and the ablation studies show that it does indeed help with the overall performance.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant challenge in materials science: efficient and accurate segmentation of micrographs with limited labeled data. The interactive segmentation paradigm is itself well-established, but this work adds the advantage of efficient generalisation, enabling high-quality results across a range of material types with limited per-application training. The claim of "maybe you don't need a U-net" is strong, but the paper shows that this is a suitable alternative to deep convolutional networks, especially when labelled data is scarce. The efficiency of the proposed approach (being usable on a laptop GPU) is also beneficial to materials scientists.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:**  The primary strength is the computational efficiency of the convolutional upsampling, enabling its use in real-time interactive segmentation on resource-constrained hardware.
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The method significantly reduces the need for densely labeled training data, a major bottleneck in materials science.
    *   **Generalization:** The use of pretrained ViT features allows for good generalization across different material systems and imaging modalities, reducing the need for application-specific training.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes experiments across various real-world microscopy datasets and benchmark datasets. The comparisons against other upsampling methods and against a full CNN (MicroNet) are useful. The ablation studies are well-motivated and provide insights into the importance of different components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on FeatUp:**  The reliance on FeatUp to generate high-resolution ground truth features is a limitation. While the authors address distribution shifts caused by FeatUp by preprocessing the input features with the PCA from it, this introduces additional complexity and dependence on an external algorithm. It also does mean the choice of upsampling algorithm is limited by its capability to create the ground truths for it, which is not fully ideal.
    *   **Positional Bias:** The authors acknowledge the positional bias inherent in ViT features.
    *   **Natural Image Bias:** The pre-trained ViT backbones are trained on natural images, which may not be optimal for all types of micrographs. This is acknowledged by the authors.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to SAM:** Given the widespread use of SAM (Segment Anything Model), it might have been interesting to compare it to SAM models to see where the differences lie.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and the method is explained clearly with diagrams. However, the number of ablation studies and detailed experimental results can make it a bit difficult to follow at times. It might be useful to provide more high-level takeaways from each experimental section.

**Overall:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of materials image segmentation by introducing an efficient and data-efficient method for leveraging the power of foundation models. The approach is well-motivated, thoroughly evaluated, and addresses a real-world problem in the materials science community. While the dependence on FeatUp and potential natural image bias are weaknesses, the benefits of efficiency and generalization outweigh these limitations. It's a strong, practical method for a very common problem.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HealthProcessAI: A Technical Framework and Proof-of-Concept for LLM-Enhanced Healthcare Process Mining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21540v1)
- **Authors**: Eduardo Illueca-Fernandez, Kaile Chen, Fernando Seoane, Farhad Abtahi
- **Abstract**: Process mining has emerged as a powerful analytical technique for understanding complex healthcare workflows. However, its application faces significant barriers, including technical complexity, a lack of standardized approaches, and limited access to practical training resources. We introduce HealthProcessAI, a GenAI framework designed to simplify process mining applications in healthcare and epidemiology by providing a comprehensive wrapper around existing Python (PM4PY) and R (bupaR) libraries. To address unfamiliarity and improve accessibility, the framework integrates multiple Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated process map interpretation and report generation, helping translate technical analyses into outputs that diverse users can readily understand. We validated the framework using sepsis progression data as a proof-of-concept example and compared the outputs of five state-of-the-art LLM models through the OpenRouter platform. To test its functionality, the framework successfully processed sepsis data across four proof-of-concept scenarios, demonstrating robust technical performance and its capability to generate reports through automated LLM analysis. LLM evaluation using five independent LLMs as automated evaluators revealed distinct model strengths: Claude Sonnet-4 and Gemini 2.5-Pro achieved the highest consistency scores (3.79/4.0 and 3.65/4.0) when evaluated by automated LLM assessors. By integrating multiple Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated interpretation and report generation, the framework addresses widespread unfamiliarity with process mining outputs, making them more accessible to clinicians, data scientists, and researchers. This structured analytics and AI-driven interpretation combination represents a novel methodological advance in translating complex process mining results into potentially actionable insights for healthcare applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and rigorous critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "HealthProcessAI," a GenAI framework designed to simplify process mining applications in healthcare and epidemiology. It acts as a wrapper around existing Python (PM4PY) and R (bupaR) libraries, enhancing accessibility by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated process map interpretation and report generation.  HealthProcessAI incorporates modules for data loading, process mining, LLM integration, advanced analytics, report orchestration, and validation. The framework was validated using sepsis progression data, comparing the outputs of five state-of-the-art LLM models through the OpenRouter platform. The study focuses on technical exploration and demonstrates the framework's ability to process sepsis data across four proof-of-concept scenarios and generate reports through automated LLM analysis. The evaluation identified Claude Sonnet-4 and Gemini 2.5-Pro as achieving the highest consistency scores from automated LLM assessors. The authors conclude that HealthProcessAI provides a standardized framework that lowers technical and training barriers, making process mining outputs more accessible and potentially actionable for healthcare applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Clear Need:** The paper directly tackles a significant bottleneck in healthcare process mining: the difficulty for non-technical users to interpret complex outputs.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:**  HealthProcessAI presents a well-structured, modular architecture encompassing the entire process mining workflow, from data preparation to LLM-enhanced interpretation. This is a significant improvement over tools that focus solely on one aspect of the process.
    *   **Multi-LLM Integration:** The integration of multiple LLMs through OpenRouter is a valuable feature. It allows users to leverage the strengths of different models and potentially mitigate biases.
    *   **Automated Evaluation:**  The use of LLMs to evaluate other LLMs is a novel approach to addressing the scalability challenges of assessing output quality.
    *   **Educational Focus:** The explicit focus on education and accessibility through documentation and step-by-step tutorials is a commendable aspect, likely to increase adoption.
    *   **Emphasis on open source:** The commitment to opensource code can facilitate innovation and replication in a community environment.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Clinical Validation:** A major limitation is the absence of clinical validation.  While the technical functionality is demonstrated, the real-world impact on patient care and clinical decision-making remains unproven. The automated evaluation is a good starting point but does not replace expert clinical review. The paper states the outputs are not "clinically validated by healthcare professionals," a critical omission.
    *   **Limited Scope of Validation Cases:** Using only sepsis data and CKD as the proof-of-concept examples is narrow. The generalizability of the framework to other clinical domains is unclear.
    *   **LLM Evaluation Concerns:** The LLM-based evaluation of LLM outputs is a circular process. Although it presents a useful direction for automation and scalability, it is limited to aspects of the LLM itself and does not truly consider clinical soundness, and it presents bias. A reliance on other models to grade the LLM analysis could be a slippery slope for objective soundness. This method could be helpful for quickly identifying model inconsistencies, it does not test the output in a real-world manner.
    *   **Statistical Power:** The small sample size (only 4 test cases per model and 20 reports generated) limits the statistical power of the comparative analysis, as acknowledged by the authors. This means the conclusions about which LLMs perform best should be viewed as preliminary.
    *   **Overclaiming or Lack of Originality:** Some sections of the paper describe well-established healthcare informatics approaches as novel. This detracts from the truly innovative aspects.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper presents a valuable integration of process mining with LLMs to enhance interpretability in healthcare, a relatively unexplored area. The multi-LLM approach and automated evaluation system are innovative.
    *   The educational framework component is significant for promoting wider adoption of process mining in clinical settings.
    *   However, the lack of clinical validation and limited scope of validation cases somewhat diminish the immediate practical impact. The real-world clinical applications of the tool have yet to be realized.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the combination of strengths and weaknesses, a score of **6** is warranted. The paper demonstrates a promising technical framework with clear potential to address a significant problem in healthcare process mining. The multi-LLM approach, automated evaluation, and educational focus are valuable contributions. However, the lack of rigorous clinical validation and limited scope of the validation cases prevent a higher score. The paper is a good starting point for future research, but more work is needed to demonstrate its real-world impact. Without a clinical study, a higher score is difficult to justify.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Complete Gaussian Splats from a Single Image with Denoising Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21542v1)
- **Authors**: Ziwei Liao, Mohamed Sayed, Steven L. Waslander, Sara Vicente, Daniyar Turmukhambetov, Michael Firman
- **Abstract**: Gaussian splatting typically requires dense observations of the scene and can fail to reconstruct occluded and unobserved areas. We propose a latent diffusion model to reconstruct a complete 3D scene with Gaussian splats, including the occluded parts, from only a single image during inference. Completing the unobserved surfaces of a scene is challenging due to the ambiguity of the plausible surfaces. Conventional methods use a regression-based formulation to predict a single "mode" for occluded and out-of-frustum surfaces, leading to blurriness, implausibility, and failure to capture multiple possible explanations. Thus, they often address this problem partially, focusing either on objects isolated from the background, reconstructing only visible surfaces, or failing to extrapolate far from the input views. In contrast, we propose a generative formulation to learn a distribution of 3D representations of Gaussian splats conditioned on a single input image. To address the lack of ground-truth training data, we propose a Variational AutoReconstructor to learn a latent space only from 2D images in a self-supervised manner, over which a diffusion model is trained. Our method generates faithful reconstructions and diverse samples with the ability to complete the occluded surfaces for high-quality 360-degree renderings.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary and a critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for completing 3D scenes represented as Gaussian splats from a single RGB image.  The key innovation lies in using a latent diffusion model trained on a latent space learned by a Variational AutoReconstructor (VAR).  The VAR is trained using only 2D posed images, overcoming the need for ground-truth 3D Gaussian splat data.  Skip connections are used within the VAR to preserve high-frequency details, and classifier-free guidance is used in the diffusion model to control the trade-off between fidelity to the input image and diversity of the generated completions. The results on CO3D and RealEstate10K datasets demonstrate that the proposed method can generate sharper, more complete, and more diverse 3D scene reconstructions compared to existing regression-based approaches, including filling in occluded or unseen areas with plausible content. The method aims for real-time performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper exhibits a decent degree of novelty, combining several existing ideas in a non-trivial way. The core novelty resides in the specific architecture of the VAR designed to learn a latent space suitable for diffusion modeling of Gaussian splats *directly from posed 2D images*. This bypasses the usual need for either dense multi-view observations or pre-existing 3D ground truth data, addressing a practical bottleneck in the application of Gaussian splatting to single-view scenarios. While latent diffusion models and Gaussian splatting are established techniques, their integration with a self-supervised VAR specifically tailored for this task is a novel contribution. The use of skip connections and classifier-free guidance, while not individually novel, are crucial for achieving the reported results and contribute to the overall system's efficacy. It is, however, important to note that similar approaches have been explored on NeRFs and gaussian splatting which reduces the amount of novelty.

*Significance:*

The paper addresses a significant problem in 3D scene reconstruction: the need for dense or expensive 3D supervision. Single-view reconstruction is a long-standing challenge, and the demonstrated ability to complete plausible 3D scenes, especially in occluded regions, has clear applications in areas like augmented reality, robotics, and content creation. By enabling Gaussian splatting from a single image, the method lowers the barrier to entry for using this powerful rendering technique. The "real-time" claim is crucial for applications needing interaction or on-the-fly processing. The ability to generate *diverse* completions is another valuable aspect.

*Strengths:*

*   **Addresses a Practical Problem:**  The single-view constraint is a common real-world limitation.
*   **Self-Supervised Learning:**  The VAR eliminates the need for expensive 3D ground truth data.
*   **Complete Scene Reconstruction:** The method handles both foreground and background, unlike some prior approaches.
*   **Generative Approach:** Enables sampling of diverse, plausible scene completions.
*   **Real-Time Potential:** The claimed real-time performance is significant.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The paper presents compelling quantitative and qualitative comparisons against state-of-the-art methods. The ablation studies provide insight into the importance of different components.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Generalization Assessment:** While the CO3D and RealEstate10K datasets are challenging, there is no broad generalization test with other datasets, or a discussion on the limitations of the approach for different scene types. More diverse sets of data are needed.
*   **Limited Architectural Novelty:** Although the integration is important, individual architectural components have origins elsewhere.
*   **Dependency on Dataset Bias:** The quality of the scene completion heavily relies on the prior learned from the training dataset.  If the training data doesn't represent the target environment well, the completions could be unrealistic or artifact-ridden. This is a common limitation of generative models, but it needs to be acknowledged.
*   **Lack of User Studies:** A user study evaluating the plausibility or usefulness of the generated diverse samples would further strengthen the paper. Do users find these samples realistically different, or does the variance fall into unimpressive, unconvincing changes.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence the field by making Gaussian splatting more accessible for single-view scenarios. It might spark further research into self-supervised learning of 3D scene representations and the application of diffusion models for 3D completion tasks. Other generative models and representations may be tried.

*Justification for Score:*

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution by enabling Gaussian splatting from a single image using a self-supervised approach. The approach addresses a practical problem and demonstrates promising results compared to existing methods. The paper is well-written, the experiments are well-designed, and the results are clearly presented. However, the novelty lies more in the clever integration of existing ideas than in fundamentally new theoretical insights. There are also limits to its generalization and dependency on dataset bias.

Score: 7

### Summarize-Exemplify-Reflect: Data-driven Insight Distillation Empowers LLMs for Few-shot Tabular Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21561v1)
- **Authors**: Yifei Yuan, Jiatong Li, Weijia Zhang, Mohammad Aliannejadi, Evangelos Kanoulas, Renjun Hu
- **Abstract**: Recent studies show the promise of large language models (LLMs) for few-shot tabular classification but highlight challenges due to the variability in structured data. To address this, we propose distilling data into actionable insights to enable robust and effective classification by LLMs. Drawing inspiration from human learning processes, we introduce InsightTab, an insight distillation framework guided by principles of divide-and-conquer, easy-first, and reflective learning. Our approach integrates rule summarization, strategic exemplification, and insight reflection through deep collaboration between LLMs and data modeling techniques. The obtained insights enable LLMs to better align their general knowledge and capabilities with the particular requirements of specific tabular tasks. We extensively evaluate InsightTab on nine datasets. The results demonstrate consistent improvement over state-of-the-art methods. Ablation studies further validate the principle-guided distillation process, while analyses emphasize InsightTab's effectiveness in leveraging labeled data and managing bias.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces InsightTab, a novel framework for enhancing the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in few-shot tabular classification.  The core idea is to distill actionable insights from the training data, enabling LLMs to better adapt to specific tabular tasks.  Inspired by human learning processes, InsightTab follows the principles of divide-and-conquer, easy-first, and reflective learning.  It integrates rule summarization, strategic exemplification, and insight reflection, leveraging both LLMs and traditional data modeling techniques. The framework involves grouping similar data samples, ranking examples based on prediction difficulty, and summarizing rules in natural language. These distilled insights are then incorporated into a multifaceted serialization prompt for the LLM. Experimental results on nine diverse datasets demonstrate the consistent superiority of InsightTab over state-of-the-art methods. Ablation studies and in-depth analyses further validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework in leveraging labeled data and mitigating biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty of the paper lies in its integration of data modeling insights into LLM prompting for tabular data.  While previous work has explored LLMs for tabular classification and incorporated task-specific knowledge, InsightTab presents a more structured and principle-driven approach to insight distillation. The human learning principles (divide-and-conquer, easy-first, and reflective learning) provide a sound foundation for the framework. The combination of data modeling techniques (like XGBoost for grouping and ranking) with LLM summarization is innovative.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a significant challenge in applying LLMs to structured data: effectively transferring knowledge from general-purpose models to specific tabular tasks with limited labeled data. Few-shot learning in tabular settings is crucial in many real-world applications, where labeling large datasets is expensive. The demonstrated improvements over existing methods indicate the potential of InsightTab to advance the state of the art in this area.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Principled approach:** The framework is grounded in well-defined principles inspired by human learning, making it more interpretable and adaptable.
    * **Integration of techniques:** The synergy between LLMs and data modeling techniques enables effective insight distillation.
    * **Strong experimental results:** Consistent improvements across multiple datasets demonstrate the robustness of the proposed method.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** Ablation studies and bias analyses provide a deeper understanding of the framework's components and behavior.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The framework involves several steps and components, potentially increasing the implementation complexity.
    * **Dependency on a powerful LLM:** The method relies on a strong LLM (e.g., gpt-4-turbo) for rule summarization, which could limit its accessibility due to API costs or resource requirements. While the paper mentions lowering serving costs by applying the rules to a smaller LLM, this angle could be further explored.
    * **Limited analysis of failure cases:**  While the paper presents bias analyses, a more in-depth analysis of failure cases could reveal further insights into the limitations of the approach.
    * **Prompt engineering:** Like all LLM-based methods, InsightTab involves prompt engineering.  Although the prompts are included in the appendix, the sensitivity of the results to prompt variations could be discussed further.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    * **Inspire new approaches to LLM prompting:**  The idea of distilling actionable insights from data can be applied to other tasks and domains.
    * **Encourage the integration of data modeling techniques with LLMs:** The paper demonstrates the benefits of combining the strengths of both types of approaches.
    * **Provide a benchmark for few-shot tabular classification:**  The comprehensive evaluation and public availability of code and data will facilitate future comparisons.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to few-shot tabular classification by strategically distilling insights from data and integrating them with LLM prompting. The method is well-motivated, grounded in sound learning principles, and demonstrates significant improvements over existing methods.  While the method has limitations related to complexity and reliance on large LLMs, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The detailed analyses and insights provided contribute significantly to understanding how LLMs can be effectively applied to tabular data. The potential for impact in the field of LLM-based data analysis is substantial.

### How Well Do Vision--Language Models Understand Cities? A Comparative Study on Spatial Reasoning from Street-View Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21565v1)
- **Authors**: Juneyoung Ro, Namwoo Kim, Yoonjin Yoon
- **Abstract**: Effectively understanding urban scenes requires fine-grained spatial reasoning about objects, layouts, and depth cues. However, how well current vision-language models (VLMs), pretrained on general scenes, transfer these abilities to urban domain remains underexplored. To address this gap, we conduct a comparative study of three off-the-shelf VLMs-BLIP-2, InstructBLIP, and LLaVA-1.5-evaluating both zero-shot performance and the effects of fine-tuning with a synthetic VQA dataset specific to urban scenes. We construct such dataset from segmentation, depth, and object detection predictions of street-view images, pairing each question with LLM-generated Chain-of-Thought (CoT) answers for step-by-step reasoning supervision. Results show that while VLMs perform reasonably well in zero-shot settings, fine-tuning with our synthetic CoT-supervised dataset substantially boosts performance, especially for challenging question types such as negation and counterfactuals. This study introduces urban spatial reasoning as a new challenge for VLMs and demonstrates synthetic dataset construction as a practical path for adapting general-purpose models to specialized domains.
- **Summary**: Okay, here is a summary and evaluation of the paper "How Well Do Vision–Language Models Understand Cities? A Comparative Study on Spatial Reasoning from Street-View Images":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how well vision-language models (VLMs) can understand urban scenes by evaluating their spatial reasoning abilities on street-view images. The authors conduct a comparative study of three VLMs: BLIP-2, InstructBLIP, and LLaVA-1.5. They create a synthetic Visual Question Answering (VQA) dataset specific to urban scenes, using segmentation, depth estimation, and object detection predictions from street-view images. The dataset includes Chain-of-Thought (CoT) answers, generated by an LLM, to provide step-by-step reasoning supervision. The study evaluates both zero-shot performance and the effect of fine-tuning on the synthetic dataset. The results demonstrate that while VLMs perform reasonably well in zero-shot settings, fine-tuning with the synthetic CoT-supervised data significantly improves performance, especially for challenging question types such as negation and counterfactuals. The paper positions urban spatial reasoning as a new challenge for VLMs and demonstrates the potential of synthetic datasets for adapting general-purpose models to specialized domains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Contribution:** The paper addresses an important and relatively under-explored problem: the ability of VLMs to understand the nuances of urban environments and spatial relationships within them.
    *   **Dataset:** The generation of a synthetic VQA dataset specifically tailored for urban street-view images is a notable contribution.  The use of segmentation, depth estimation, and object detection outputs to create ground truth is a practical approach, especially considering the difficulties in obtaining large, high-quality human-annotated datasets.  The inclusion of CoT rationales provides a valuable signal for step-by-step reasoning during fine-tuning.
    *   **Methodology:** The comparative study itself is well-structured, evaluating a range of VLMs with varying architectures and training approaches. The analysis of zero-shot vs. fine-tuned performance, along with breakdowns by question type, provides valuable insights.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Impact:** The findings have the potential to influence how VLMs are applied to urban planning, navigation, and public space design. By highlighting the challenges and demonstrating a practical adaptation approach, the paper encourages further research in this area.
    *   **Future Work:** The identification of model-specific strengths and weaknesses is also valuable for future research, guiding the selection and fine-tuning of models for specific urban scene understanding tasks.
    *   **Limitations:**  The reliance on *synthetic* data introduces a potential limitation. While the authors validate the quality of the dataset with human evaluation, there is still a risk that the models learn biases present in the pretrained prediction models (segmentation, depth, etc.).  The study could be strengthened by evaluating the models on a small, carefully human-annotated real-world dataset as well. The scale of the fine-tuning is another limitation; a larger or more diverse synthetic dataset might lead to even greater improvements. It is also unclear the degree of variability in the "urban" environments evaluated, as most street view imagery presents similar challenges.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The paper focuses on a limited number of VLMs. Expanding the study to include other recent architectures would enhance the generalizability of the findings. The urban environments were only from 5 cities. A broader range of global cities could strengthen the results.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined research questions.
    *   Clearly described methodology.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with both quantitative and qualitative analysis.
    *   The modular QA generation pipeline provides a reproducible and scalable approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on synthetic data.
    *   Limited number of VLMs and urban environments evaluated.
    *   Potential for catastrophic forgetting during fine-tuning.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by exploring the application of VLMs to urban scene understanding and spatial reasoning. The creation of the synthetic VQA dataset with CoT rationales is a particularly valuable contribution, addressing the challenge of obtaining large, high-quality annotated datasets for this domain. The comparative study provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different VLMs, and the potential benefits of fine-tuning. However, the reliance on synthetic data and the limitations in the scale and diversity of the evaluation prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.  While the research is well executed and the findings are promising, some reservations exist about the extent of novelty. The experiments are more on the empirical side, and limited theoretical depth is present.

### A Survey on Current Trends and Recent Advances in Text Anonymization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21587v1)
- **Authors**: Tobias Deußer, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Armin Berger, Max Hahnbück, Christian Bauckhage, Rafet Sifa
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of textual data containing sensitive personal information across various domains requires robust anonymization techniques to protect privacy and comply with regulations, while preserving data usability for diverse and crucial downstream tasks. This survey provides a comprehensive overview of current trends and recent advances in text anonymization techniques. We begin by discussing foundational approaches, primarily centered on Named Entity Recognition, before examining the transformative impact of Large Language Models, detailing their dual role as sophisticated anonymizers and potent de-anonymization threats. The survey further explores domain-specific challenges and tailored solutions in critical sectors such as healthcare, law, finance, and education. We investigate advanced methodologies incorporating formal privacy models and risk-aware frameworks, and address the specialized subfield of authorship anonymization. Additionally, we review evaluation frameworks, comprehensive metrics, benchmarks, and practical toolkits for real-world deployment of anonymization solutions. This review consolidates current knowledge, identifies emerging trends and persistent challenges, including the evolving privacy-utility trade-off, the need to address quasi-identifiers, and the implications of LLM capabilities, and aims to guide future research directions for both academics and practitioners in this field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided survey paper on text anonymization:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive survey of text anonymization techniques, covering foundational methods based on Named Entity Recognition (NER), the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) – both as anonymizers and de-anonymization threats – domain-specific challenges in healthcare, law, finance, and education, advanced privacy-preserving techniques, and authorship anonymization. It also examines evaluation frameworks, metrics, and available toolkits. The survey consolidates current knowledge, highlights emerging trends, and addresses persistent challenges in the field, such as the privacy-utility trade-off and the implications of LLM capabilities, aiming to guide future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Comprehensive Coverage:**  The survey covers a wide range of text anonymization techniques, from traditional NER-based approaches to more recent LLM-driven methods and specialized techniques. Its inclusion of specific domains, privacy models, and authorship anonymization provides a well-rounded perspective.
    * **Timeliness:** The survey addresses the critical and rapidly evolving role of LLMs in text anonymization, which is a significant and timely topic. It clearly articulates the dual nature of LLMs as both powerful tools and potential threats.
    * **Well-Organized and Structured:** The paper is logically organized, making it easy to follow.  The clear sections on domain-specific challenges and solutions are particularly valuable.
    * **Emphasis on Evaluation:** The focus on evaluation frameworks, metrics, and benchmarks is crucial, highlighting the importance of rigorously assessing anonymization techniques.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The inclusion of anonymization toolkits and systems enhances the practical value of the survey for researchers and practitioners.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Depth in Some Areas:**  While the breadth is impressive, the depth of discussion in certain areas could be improved. For instance, the detailed mathematical foundations of certain privacy models (e.g., differential privacy) are not deeply explored, which might limit the understanding of readers not already familiar with these models.
    * **Limited Critical Comparison:**  While the survey presents different approaches, it could benefit from more explicit critical comparisons between them.  For example, a more direct head-to-head comparison of the strengths and weaknesses of NER-based vs. LLM-based methods in different scenarios would be valuable. A more nuanced analysis of the performance across these different paradigms would greatly increase the paper's value.
    * **Potential for Overlap with Existing Surveys:** A critical aspect of a survey is its ability to consolidate and advance existing knowledge. The paper needs to explicitly demonstrate how it distinguishes itself from existing surveys on text anonymization (if any) in terms of scope, depth, or timeliness. This could include emphasizing newly emerging areas or a more focused lens.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

    * The true novelty lies in the comprehensive integration of LLMs' dual role within the existing text anonymization landscape. Surveys need to be forward-looking, highlighting the newest frontiers and the potential paths for future research. By offering perspective into all the core areas, the impact is significantly enhanced.
    * The paper is beneficial in highlighting the challenges and opportunities presented by LLMs. It serves as a useful reference for researchers and practitioners entering the field. However, its influence depends on its ability to stay updated with the rapid advancements in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The survey paper is a solid and valuable contribution to the field of text anonymization. It provides a well-structured overview of existing techniques, highlights the important role of LLMs, and emphasizes the importance of evaluation. Its comprehensive coverage and timeliness make it a useful resource for researchers and practitioners. However, its impact could be further enhanced by deeper critical comparisons and a more detailed examination of the underlying mathematical foundations of privacy-preserving techniques, as well as emphasizing the novel viewpoints of the paper compared to other potential survey work.
